iy The PREFACE.

"was, that I made a judicious Choice and proper Diſpofition of
the Paſſages I extracted. A Mixture of ſo many different Sub-
jets, and ſuch a Variety of Thoughts upon them, may poſſibly not

one entire Subject; but certainly it will divert and amuſe him

to any Length of Reading. 1 detain him therefore only to ac-
quaint him, why it is made a Part of this Book, and how ſer-
wiceable it may be to the main Deſign of it.

Hawing drawn up Rules for making Verſes, and a Dictionary
of Rhymes, which are the mechanick Tools of a Poet ; I came in
the next place to confider, what other human Aid could be of -
fer'd him, a Genius and Judgment not being mine to give. Now
I imagin'd that a Man might have both theſe, and yet ſome-
times, for the ſake of a Syllable or two, more or leſs, to give a
Verſe its true Meaſure, be at a ſtand for Epithets and Synonyma's,
with which I have ſeen Books of this Nature in ſeveral Lan-

guages plentifully furniſhed.

Now, tho' I have differ'd from them in Method, yet I am of
opinion this Collection may ſerve to the ſame End, with equal
Prof and greater Pleaſure to the Reader. For, what are E-
pithets, but Adjettives that denote and expreſs the Qualities of
the Subſtantives to which they are join'd ? as Purple, Roſy,
Smiling, Dewy, Morning : Dim, Gloomy, Silent, Night.
What Synonyma's, but Words of a like Signification ? as Fear,
Dread, Terrour, Conſternation, Affright, Diſmay, c. Are
they not then naturally to be ſought for in the Deſcriptions
of Perſons and Things? And can wwe not better judge by a
Piece of Painting, how beautifully Colours may be e than
by ſeeing the ſame ſeveral Colours ſcatter'd without Deſign on a
Table? When you are at à boſs therefore for proper Epithets or
Synonyma's, look into this Alphabetical Collection for any Ward
under which the Subject of your Thought may moft probably
be rang d, and you-wvill find what have been employ'd by our
beft Writers, and in what manner.

It would have been as eaſy a Taſk for me, as it has been to
ethers before me, to have threaded tedious Bead-rolls of Synony-
ma's and Epithets together, and put them by themſekues : But
*when they fland alone they appear bald, i 1 uncouth, and

But befides, to confeſt a Secret, I am very unwilling it hould
be laid to my Charge, that I have furniſb d Tools and given a
Temptation of Verifying, to ſuch as in ſpite of Art and Nature
rndertake to be Poets; and who miſtake their Fondneſs to Rhyme,
or Neceſſity of Writing, for a true Genius of Poetry, and lawful
Call from Apollo. Such Debaſers of Rhyme and Dablers in
Poetry would do well to conſider, that a Man would juſtly de-
ſerde a higher Eſteem in the World, by being a good Maſon or
Shoe-maker, or by excelling in any other Art that his Talent in-
clines him to, and that is uſeful to Mankind, than by being an
indifferent or ſecond-Rate Poet. Such have no Claim to that
Divine Appellation:

Neque enim concludere Verſum
Dixeris eſſe ſatis ; Neque, fi quis ſcribat, uti nos,
Sermoni propriora, putes hunc eſſe Poetam.
Ingenium cui fit, cui Mens divinior, atque Os
Magna ſonaturum, des Nominis hujus Honorem. Horat.

I have inſerted not only Similies, Alluſions, Characters, and
Deſcriptions, but alſo the moſt Natural and Sublime Thoughts
of our Modern Poets on all Subjects whatever. I ſay, of our
Modern ; for tho" ſome of the Ancient, as Chaucer, Spencer,
and others, have not been excell'd, perhaps not eguall d, by any
that have ſucceeded them, either in Juſtneſt of Deſcription, or
in Propriety and Greatneſs of Thought; yet their Language is
now become ſo antiquated and obſelete, that moſt Readers of our
Age have no Ear for them : And this is the Reaſon that the
good Shakeſpear himſelf is not fo frequently cited in this Col-
leAion, as he would otherwiſe deſerve to be.

ENGLISH VERSE.

CC. A-P. 1
Of the Strufture of Engliſh Verſes.

HE Structure of our Verſes, whether Blank, or in

Rhyme, conſiſts in a certain Number of Syllables ;
not in Feet compos'd of long and ſhort Syllables, as the
Verſes of the Greeks and Romans. And though ſome oy 2
ous Perſons formerly puzzled themſelves in preſcribing Rules
for the Quantity of Engl Syllables, and, in Imitation of
the Latins, compos'd Verſes by the Meaſure of Spendet,
Da&yls, c. yet the Succeſs of their Undertaking has fully
evinc'd the Vainneſs of their Attempt, and given 8 to
ſuſpect they had not throughly 1 what the Genius of
our Language would bear; nor reflected that each Tongue
has its peculiar Beauties, and that what is agrecable and na-
tural to one, is very oftendiſagreeable, nay, inconſiſtent with
an-

ENGLISH VERSE. 9

another. But that Deſign being now wholly exploded, it
is ſufficient to have mention'd it.

Our Verſes then conſiſt in a certain Number of Syllables ;
but the Verſes of double Rhyme require a Syllable more than
thoſe of ſingle Rhyme. Thus in a Poem whoſe Verſes con-

fiſt of ten Syllables, thoſe of the ſame Poem that are accent-

ed on the laſt ſave one, which we call Verſes of double
Rhyme, muſt have eleven; as may be ſeen by theſe Verſes,

A Mar fo wadions that he ſcem'd to be

Not one, but all Mankind's Epitome :

Stiff in Opinion, always in the Wrong,

Was ev'ry thing by flarts, and 2 long;
But, in the Courſe of one revolving Moon,

Was Fidler, Chymiſi, Stateſman, and Buffon:
Then all for Women, Painting, Rhyming, Drinking,
Beſides Ten thouſand Freaks that dy'd inThinking,
Praifing and Railing awere his uſual T hemes,

And both, to ſhew his Judgment, in Extremes.

So over-wiolent, or over-civid, |

That every Man with him was God or Devil, Dryd..

Where the 4 Verſes that are accented on the laſt ſave one
have 11 Syllables : the others, accented on the laſt, but 10.

require 9; as,
When hard Words, * and Fears,
Set Folks together by the Lars;
And made em fight, like mad, or drunk,
Fer Dame Religion as for Punk ;;
Whoſe Honeſty they all durſt ſwear for,
Tho” not a Man of em knew wherefore :
Then did Sir Knight abandon Davelling, |
And out he rode a Colonelling. © Hud..
In a Poem whoſe Verſes conſiſt of 7, the double Rhymes
require 8; as,
Ati Verſe is ſiſter far
Than the downy Feathers are
Of my Wings, or of my Arrows,
Of my Mother's Doves or Sparrows. Cowl..

This muſt alſo be obſerved in Blank Verſe; as,

Welcome, thou worthy Partner of my Laurel: !'
Thau Brather of my Choice Band more facred'

S.CT... I. |
Of the ſeveral Sorts of Verſes ; and, firſt, of thoſe
of Ten Syllables : Of the due Obſervation of the
Accents, and of the Pauſe.

Off Poetry admits for the moſt part but of Three Sorts
of Verſes ; that is to ſay, of Verſes of 10, 8, or 7
Syllables : Thoſe of 4, 6,9, 11, 12, and 14, are .. ane
employ'd in Maſks and Operas, and in the Stanzas of Lyri
and Pindarick ' Odes, and we have few entire Poems com-
pos'd in any of'thofe Sorts of Verſes. Thoſe of 12 and 14.
Syllables are frequently inſerted in our Poems in Heroick
Verſe, and when rightly made uſe of, carry a iar Grace
with them. See the next Section towards the End.

The Verſes of 10 Syllables, which are our Heroick, are
us'd in Heroick Poems, in Tragedies, Comedies, Paſtorals,
Elegies, and ſometimes in Burleſque. x

In theſe Verſes two Things are chiefly to be conſider d;

1. The Seat of the Accent ; | :
2. The Pauſe.

For 'tis not enough that Verſes have their juſt Number
of Syllables ; the true Harmony of them depends on a due
Obſervation of the Accent and Pauſe. Th

e

it

ENGLISRH VERSE. II

The Accent is an Elevation or a falling of the Voice on
a certain Syllable of a Word.

The Pauſe is a Reſt or Stop that is made in pronouncing
the Verſe, and that divides it, as it were, into two Parts;
each of which is call'd an Hemiſtich, or Half- Verſe.

But this Diviſion is not always equal, that is to ſay, one
of the Half. Verſes does not always contain the ſame Num-
ber of Syllables as the other : And this Inequality proceeds
from the Seat of the Accent that is ſtrongeſt, and prevails
moſt in the firſt Half-Verſe: For the Pauſe muſt be obſerv'd
at the End of the Word where ſuch Accents happen to be,
or at the End of the following Word. -

Now in a Verſe of 10 Syllables this Accent muſt be either
on the 2d, 4th, or 6th ; which produces 5 ſeveral Pauſes, that
is to ſay, at the 3d, 4th, 5th, 6th, or 7th Syllable of the Verſe:

For,

When it happens to be on the 2d, the Pauſe will be either
at the 3d or 4th. |

At the 3d, in two Manners :

1. When the Syllable accented happens to be the laſt
ſave one of a Word 3 as, g

As buſy--as intentive Emmets are ;
Or Cities--awhom unlook'd fer Sieges ſcare. Dav.

2. Or when the Accent is on the laſt of a Word, and the
next a Monoſyllable, whoſe Conſtruction is govern'd by that
on which the Accent is; as,

Deſpiſe it--and more noble Thoughts purſue. Dryd.

When the Accent falls on the 2d Syllable of the Verſe, and
the laſt ſave two of a Word, the Pauſe will be at the 4th; as,

He meditates--his abſent Enemy. Dryd.

When the Accent is on the 4th of a Verſe, the Pauſe will
be either at the ſame Syllable, or at the 5th or 6th.

At the ſame, when the Syllable of the Accent happens to
be the laſt of a Word; as, |

Such huge Extremes--inhabit thy great Mind,
God. lile, unmo vd, - and yet, like Woman, kind. Wall.

At the 5th in 2 Manners: |

I2 Rules for making
1. When it happens to be the laſt ſave one of a Word; as,

Like bright Aurora--whoje refulgent Ray

Foretells the Ferwour--of enſuing Day;

And warns the Shepherd--with his Flocks, retreat

To leafy Shadows--from the threaten'd Heat. Wall.

2. Or the laſt of the Word, if the next be a Monoſyllable
govern'd by it; as,

So freſh the Wound is--and the Grief fo vt. Wall.

At the 6th, when the Syllable of the Accent happens to
be the laſt ſave two of a Word; as,

T hoſe Seeds of Luxury--Debate, and Pride. Wall.

Laſtly, When the Accent is on the 6th Syllable of the Verſe,
the Pauſe will be either at the ſame Syllable or at the 7th.

At the ſame, when the Syllable of the Accent happens to
be the laſt of a Word ; as, , |

She meditates Revenge--reſolv'd to die. Wall.

At the 7th in two Manners :
1. When it happens to be the laſt ſave one of a Word; as,

Nor when the War is over, is it Peace. Dryd.
Mirrors are taught te flatter,--but our Springs. Wall.

2. Or the laſt of a Word, if the following one be a Mono-

ſyllable whoſe Conſtruction depends on the preceding Word
on which the Accent is ; as, a

And fince Te could not ſave her--with her dy'd. Dryd.

From all this it appears, that the Pauſe is determin'd by
the Seat of the Accent ; but if the Accents happen to be e
qually ſtrong on the ad, 4th, and 6th Syllable of aVerſe, the
Senſe and Conſtruction of the Words muſt then guide to the
Obſervation of the Pauſe, For Example: In one of the
Verſes J have cited as an Inſtance of it at the 7th Syllable,

Mirrors are taught ta flatter, but our Springs.

Mirrors are taught--to flatter, but our Springs
Preſent th' impartial Images of Things.

Which tho! it be no Violence to the Ear, yet it is to the

ing or in repeating of Verſes.

For this Reaſon it is, that the Conſtruction or Senſe ſhould
never end at a Syllable where the Pauſe ought not tobe made;
as at the 8th and ad in the two following Verſes :

Bright Heſper tawinkles from afar : Away \
My Kids !--for you have had a Feaſt to Day. Staff.

Which Verſes have nothing diſagreeable in their Structure
but the Pauſe, which in the firſt of them muſt be obferv'd at
the 8th Syllable, in the ad at the 2d; and ſo unequal a Divi-
fron can produce no true Harmony. And for this Reaſon
too, the Pauſes at the 3d and 7th Syllables, tho' not wholly to
be condemn'd, ought to be but ſparingly praQtis'd. 4

The foregoin | 25a ought indiſpenfibly to be follow'd in
all our Verſes of 10 Syllables; and the Obſervation of them,
like that of right Time in Muſick, will produce Harmony ;
the Negle& of them Harſhneſs and Diſcord ; as appears by
the following Verſes :

None think Rewards render'd worthy their Worth,
And both Lovers, both thy Diſciples avere. Dav.

In which. tho' the true Number of Syllables be obſerv'd, yet
neither of them have ſo much as the Sound of aVerſe: Now
their Diſagreeableneſs proceeds from the undue Seat of the
Accent ; 1 The firſt of them is accented on the
5th and 7th Syllables ; but if we change the Words, and re-
move the Accent to the 4th and 6th, the Verſe will become
ſmooth and eaſy ; as,

None think Rewards are equal to their Worth.

The Harſhneſs of the laſt of them proceeds from its being
accented on the 3d Syllable, which may be mended thus,
by tranſpofing only one Word;

And Lowers bath, both thy Diſciples were. .

In like manner, the following Verſes ;

From whence we conclude, that in all Verſes of 10 Syl-
lables, the moſt prevailing Accents ought to be on the ad,
4th, or 6th Syllables ; for if they are on the zd, 5th, or
7th, the Verſes will be rough and diſagreeable, as has been
prov'd by the preceding Inſtances.

In ſhort, the wrong placing of the Accent is as great a
Fault in our Verſification, as falſe Quantity was in that of
the Ancients; and therefore we ought to take equal care to
avoid it, and endeavour ſo to difpote the Words, that they
may create a certain Melody in the Ear, without Labour to
the Tongue, or Violence to the Senſe, SECT

In the Structure of theſe Verſes, as well as of thoſe of 10

cents be neither on the 3d nor 5th Syllables of them.

They alſo require a Pauſe to be obſerv'd in pronouncing

In mighty Numbers,--mighty T hings ;

The Strings will found--of nought but Love. Cowl..

The Verſes of 7 Syllables, which are called 4nacreontich,

Fill the Bowl--with roſy Wine,

Round our Temples-- Roſes tavine ;

Crown'd with Roſes --wwe contemn

The Verſes of 9 and of 11 Syllables, are of two Sorts ;

of 8 and 10 Syllables, of which Examples have already been
given : The other of thoſe that are accented on the laſt
Syllable, which are employ'd only in Compoſitions for
Muſick, and in the loweſt Sort of burleſque Poetry; the
Diſagreeableneſs of their Meaſure having wholly excluded
them from grave and ſerious Subjects. They who deſire to

ſee Examples of them, may find ſome ſcatter'd here and

there in our Maſks and Operas, and in our burleſque Writers.

Several Rules conducing to the Beauty of our Ver-

fification.

UR Poetry being very much poliſh'd and refin'd ſince
the Days of Chaucer, Spencer, and the other ancient
Poets, ſome Rules which they neglected, and that conduce
very much to the Ornaments of it, have been pra&is'd by
the beſt of the Moderns. | 4
The firſt is to avoid as much as poſſible the Concourſe of
Vowels, which occaſions a certain ill-ſounding Gaping, call'd
by the Latins Hiatus; and which they thought fo dif ble
to the Ear, that, to avoid it, whenever a Word ended in a
Vowel, and the next began with one, they never, even in
Proſe, ſounded the Vowel of the firſt Word, but loſt it in
the Pronunciation; and it is a Fault in our Poets not to do
the like, whenever our Language will admit of it.
For this Reaſon the : of * Particle The * * always to
be cut off before the Words that begin with a Vowel; as,

With weeping Eyes ſhe heard th unwelcome News, Dryd.

And itis a Fault to make The and the firſt Syllable of the
following Word two diſtinct Syllables ; as in this,

| Reftrain'd a while by the unwelcome Night. Wall,

A ſecond Sort of Hiatus, and that ought no leſs to be a-
voided, is, when a Word that ends in a Vowel that cannot
be cut off, is plac'd before one that begins with the ſame
Vowel, or one that has the like Sound ; as,

Should thy Iambicks favell into @ Book. Wall.

Te ſecond Rule is, to contract the two laſt Syllables of
the Preterperfect Tenſes of all the Verbs that will admit of
it; which are all the Regular Verbs whatſoever, except only
thoſe ending in D or T, and DE or TE. And it is a Fault
to make Amaxed of three Syllables, and Lowed of two, in-
ſtead of Amax d of two, and Loud of one,

And

And the ſecond Perſon of the Preſent and P
Tenſes of all Verbs ought to be contracted in like manner;
as, thou log, for thou love, &c.
The third Rule is, not to make uſe of ſeveral Words i in a
Verſe that begin with the ſame Letter ; as,
The Court he knew to ſtaer in Storms of State.
He in theſe Miracles Deſign diſcern d. Dav.

Yet we find an Inſtance of ſuch a Verſe in Dryden's Tranſ-
lation of the firſt Paſtoral of Virgil;

Till then a helpieſe, hopeleſs, homely Swain.
Which I am perſuaded he left not thus through .—
or Inadvertency, but with deſign to om in the Number

and Sound of the Words the Thing he deicribed, a Shepherd
in whom
Nec fpes libertatis erat, nec cura peculi.

Now how far the Sound of the H Afpirate, with which
three Feet of that Verſe begin, expreſſes the Deipair of the
Swain, let the Judicious — "5 I have taken notice of it
only to ſay, that 'tis a great Beauty in Poetry. when the
Words and Numbers are ſo difpos'd, as by their Order and
Sound to repreſent the Things deſcrib'd.

The fourth is, to avoid __ a Verſe by an Adjective
whoſe Subſlantive begins the following; as,

Some loft their quiet e FOO IR

Parents, &c. Dav.
Or, by a Prepoſition when oe Caſe it governs begins the
Verſe that follows ; as,

The daily ef] ning of our Life, ſhews
A little dying, how outright 10 dye. 9 Wall.

The fifth is, to avoid the frequent Uſe of Words of many
Syllables, which are proper enough in Proſe, but come not
into Verſe without a —— 9 — altogether diſagreeable;

particularly thoſe whoſe Accent is onthe fourth Syllable from
the the Jon, as 2

8 E T. IV.
Doubts concerning the Number of Syllables of cer-
fain Words.

HERE is no Language. whatſoover that ſo often joins
ſeveral Vowels together to make Diphthongs of them,
: as

1 Rol zs for making

as ours; this appears in our having ſeveral compos'd of three
different Vowels, as EAU and EOU in Beauteous, IOU in
Glorious, UAL in Acquaint, &c.

Now from hence may ariſe ſome Difficulties concerning the
true Pronunciation of thoſe Vowels whether they ought to
be ſounded ſeparately in two Syllables, or jointly in one.

The ancient Poets made them ſometimes of two Syllables,
ſometimes but of one, as the Meaſure of theirVerſe requir'd ;
but they are now become to be but of one, and it is a Fault
to make them of two: From whence we may draw this
general Rule ;

That whenever one Syllable of a Word ends in a Vowel,
and the next begins with one, provided the firſt of thoſe Sylla-
bles be not that on which the Word is accented, thoſe two
Syllables ought in Verſe to be contracted and made but one.

Thus Beautecus is but two Syllables, Victorieus but three

and it is a Fault in Dryden to make it four, as he has done

in this Verſe : |
Vr Arms arc on the Rhine vidorious,
To prove that this Verſe wants a Syllable of its due Mea-

. ſure, we need but add one to it ; as,

Your Arms are on the Rhine victerious now.

Where, tho' the Syllable now be added to the Verſe, it has
no more than its due Number of Syllables ; which plainly
proves it wanted it. |
But if the Accent be upon the firſt of theſe Syllables, they
cannot be contracted to make a Diphthong, but mult be
computed as two diſtin& Syllables : Thus Poet, Lion, Quiet,
and the like, muſt always be us'd as two Syllables ; Poetry,
and the like, as three. 572
And it is a Fault to make Riot, for Example, one Syllable,
as Milton has done in this Verſe,

Their Riot aſcends above the lofty Tow'rs,

The ſame Poet has in another Place made uſe of a like
Word twice in one Verſe, and made it two Syllables each
time;

With Ruin upon Ruin, Rout on Rout,

And any Ear may diſcover that this laſt Verſe has its true
Meaſure, the other not. But

But there are ſome Words that may be excepted; as Diamond,
Violet, Violent, Diadem, Hyacinth, and perhaps ſome others,
which, though they are accented upon the firſt Vowel, are
ſometimes us d but as twoSyllables; as in the following Verſes,

From Diamond Quarries heaun, and Rocks of Gold. Milt.
With Poppies, Daffodils, and Violets join d. Tate.
With vain, but violent Force their Darts they fung. Cowl.
His Ephod, Mitre, well-cut Diadem on. f Cowl.
My bluſhing Hyacinths, and my Bays I keep. Dryd.
Sometimes as three; as,
A Mount of rocky Diamond did riſe. Blac.
Hence the blue Violet and bluſhing Roſe. Blac.
And ſet ſoft Hyacinths of Iron Blue. Dryd.

When they are us'd but as two Syllables they ſuffer an
Eliſion of one of their Vowels, and are generally written thus,
Di'mond, Vi let, &c. |

This Contraction is not always made of Syllables of the
ſameWord only ; for the Particle A being placed after aWord
that ends in a Vowel, will ſometimes admit of the like Con-
traction; for Example, after the Word many; as,

Tho many a Viftim from my Folds was bought,
And many a Cheeſe to Country Markets brought. Dryd.
They many a Trophy gain d with many a Wound. Dav.

After To; as,
Can he to a Friend, to a Son ſo bloody grow ?

After They; as,
From thee, their long- nogun King, they a King defire, Cowl.
After By ; as, |
When we by a fooliſb Figure ſay.

And perhaps after ſome others. |
There are alſo other Words whoſe Syllables are ſometimes
contracted, ſometimes not; as Bower, Heaven, Prayer, Nigher,
Towards, and many more of the like Nature : But they gene-
rally ought to be us'd but as one Syllable ; and then they
ſuffer an Elifion of the Vowel that precedes their final Con-
ſonant, and ought to be written thus, Pow'r, Heaw'n, Pray'r,
Nieb'r, Toro db.

And indeed, conſidering that it has but one Vowel, it ma
ſeem abſurd to aſſert that it ought to be reckon'd two Syl-
—4 ; yet in my opinion Verſes ſeem to have a Syl-

able more than their due Meaſure, and would run better if
we took one from them; as, |
Where griſly Schiſm, raging Strife appear,
I Rheumatiſms ſend to rack the Foints.

Yet this Opinion being contrary to the conſtant Practice
of our Poets, I ſhall not preſume to advance it as a Rule for
others to follow, but leave it to be decided by ſuch as are
better Judges of poetical Numbers.

The like may be ſaid of the Terminations ASM and OSM.

SKC Tr. V.,
Of the Elifions That are allow'd in our Verfification.

UR Verſes conſiſting only of a certain Number of Syl-
lables, nothing can be of more Eaſe, or greater Uſe
to Poets, than the retaining or cutting off a Syllable from a
Verſe, according as the Meaſure of it requires; and there-
fore it is requiſite'to-treat of the Elifions that are allowable
in our Pactry, ſome of which have been already taken no-

tice of in the preceding Section. '

By Elifion, I mean the cutting. of one or more Letters
from a Word, whereby two Syllables.come to be contracted
into one ; or the taking away an entire Syllable. Now when
in a Word of more than two Syllables, which is accented on
the laſt ſave two, the Liquid R happens to be between two
Vowels, that which precedes the Liquid admits of an Eliſion.
Of this Nature are many Words in ANCE, ENCE, ENT,
ER, OUS, and RY; as Temperance, Preference, Different,
Flatterer, Amorous, Vito y: Which are Words of three Syl-
lables and often us'd as ſuch in Verſe ; but they may be alſo
contracted into two by cutting off the Vowel that precedes
the Liquid, as Temp'rance, Pręf rence, Diff rent, Flatt'rer,
An'rous, Vid ry. The like Eliſion is ſometimes us'd when
any of the other Liquids L, M or N, happen to be between
two Vowels in Words accented like the former; as Fabulous,
Enemy, Mariner, which may be contracted Fab lous, En'my,
Mar'ner. But this is not ſo frequent. |

: | Obſerve,

ENGLISH VERSE. 23

Obſerve, that I ſaid accented on the laſt ſave two ; for if
the Word be accented on the laſt ſave one, that is to ſay, on
the Vowel that precedes the Liquid, that Vowel may not be
cut of, And — it is a Fault to make, for Example,
Sonorous two Syllables, as in this Verſe;

With Son'rous Metals wak'd the drowſy Day. Blac,
Which always ought to be three, as in this; |
Senorous Metals blowing martial Saungs. Milt.

In like manner, whenever the Letter 8 happens to be be-
tween two Vowels in Words of three Syllables, accented on
the firſt, one of the Vowels may be cut off; as, Pris ner,
Bus'neſs, &c. —

Or the Letter C when tis ſounded like 8; that is to ſay,
whenever it precedes the Vowel E or I; as Med cine,
Medicine.

Or V Canſonant ; as Cov'nant, for Covenant.

To theſe may be added the Gerunds of all Verbs whoſe
Infinitives end in any of the Liquids, preceded by a Vowel or
Diphthong, and that are accented on the laſt ſave one: For
the Gerunds being form'd by adding the Syllable ING to
the Infinitive, the Liquid that was their final Letter comes
thereby to be between two Vowels ; and the Accent that
was on the laſt ſave one of the Inſinitive, comes to be on the
laſt ſave two of the Gerund: And therefore the Vowel or
Diphthong that precedes the Liquid, may be cut off ; by
means. whereof the Gerund of three Syllables-comes to be
but of two; as from Travel, Travelling, or Trav' ling; from
Endeavour, Endeawouring, or Endeav'ring, &c.

But if the Accent be on the laſt Syllable of ſuch a Verb, its
Gerund will not ſuffer ſuch an Elifion : Thus the Gerund of
Devour, muſt always be three Syllables, Devouring, not
Dew'ring ; becauſe all Derivatives ſtill retain the Accent of
their Primitives, that is, on the ſame Syllable: And the Ac-
cent always obliges theSyllable on which it is to remain entire.

The Gerunds of the Verbs in OW, accented on the laſt
ſave two, ſuffer an Eliſion of the O that precedes the W; as
FolPawing, Wall aving.

The Particle 7: > 26M of an Elifion of its Vowel before
Is, Was, Were, Will, Would ; as Tis, "Twas, *Twere, 'Twill,

'Tavould, for It is, it was, &c. F

24 Rules for making

It likewiſe ſometimes ſuffers the like Elifion when plac'{
after a Word that ends in a Vowel; as By't for By it, Ds
for Do it : Or that ends in a Conſonant after which the Let.
ter T can be pronounc'd ; as Was't for Was it, 1n't for Init, P
and the like : But this is not ſo frequent in heroic Verſe.

The Particle I may loſe its 7 after any Word that ends in
a Vowel, or in any of the Conſonants after which the Letter
S may be ſounded; as e's for be is : The Air's for the Ai
is, &c.

To (Sign of the Infinitive Mood) may loſe its O before any
Verb that begins with a Vowel; as *amaze, "undo, &c.

To (Sign of the Dative Caſe) may likewiſe loſe its O before
any Noun that begins with a Vowel ; as Air, every, &c.
But this Eliſion is not fo allowable as the former.

Are may loſe its A after the Pronouns Perſonal, We, You,
They; as We re, You're, They're: And thus it is that this Eliſion
ought to be made, and not, as ſome do, by cutting off the
final Vowels of the Pronouns Perſonal, are, T' are, Th are.

Will and Would may loſe all their firſt Letters, and retain
only their final one, after any of the Pronouns Perſonal ; as
T for I will, He'd for He would ; or after Who, who'll tot
who will, who'd for who would.

Have may loſe its two firſt Letters after I, You, We, T hey;
as Poe, You've, Neve, They ve.

Not, its two firſt Letters after Can; as Can't for Can not.

Am, its A after J; Pm for 1 am.

Us, its U after Let; Let's for Let us.

Taken, its K, Ta'en: For ſo it ought to be written, not
ta ne.

Heaven, Seven, Even, Eleven, and the Participles Driven,
Given, Thriven, and their Compounds, may loſe their lai
Vowel ; as Heawv'n, Forgiv'n, &c. See the foregoing Section, i

13.
F To theſe may be added Bow'r, Pow'r, Flos vr, Tow'r,
Show'r, for Bower, Tower, &c.

Newer, Euer, Over, may loſe their “, and are contracted
thus, Ne'er, E'er, O'er.

Some Words admit of an Elifion of their firſt Syllable ; as
"Tween, 'T wixt, ) Mong, ' Mong ft, 'Gainſt, * Bowe, Cauſe, Fort,
for Between, Betwixt, Among, Amongſt, Againſt, Above, Be-
cauſe, Before. And ſome others, that may be obſerv'd in
reading our Poets. |

Of Rhyme.

SSCT. I.
What Rhyme is, and the ſeveral Sorts of it.

HYME is a Likeneſs or Uniformity of Sound in the

Terminations of two Words: I ſay of Sound, not off
Letters; for the Office of Rhyme being to content and pleaſe
the Ear, and not the Eye, the Sound only is to be regarded,
not the Writing: Thus Maid and Perſuade, Laugh and Quap,
though they differ in Writing, rhyme very well: But Plough
and Cough, tho' their Terminations are written alike, rhyme
not at all.

In our Verſification we may obſerve three ſeveral Sorts of
Rhyme ; Single, Double, and Treble.

The ſingle Rhyme is of two Sorts: One of the Words
that are accented on the laſt Syllable: Another, of thoſe that
have their Accent on the laſt ſave two.

The Words accented on the laſt Syllable, if they end in a
Conſonant, or mute E, oblige the — to begin at the
Vowel that precedes their laſt Conſonant, and to continue to
the End of the Word: In a Conſonant; as,

Here might be ſeen, that Beauty, Wealth and Wit
And Proweſ, to the Pow'r of Love ſubmit. .

to cut A Spark of Virtue, by the deepeſt Shade
Word Of vw Zune is fairer made. Wall.

uſt begin at that Vowel of it whoſe Sound moſt prevails;

made only to that Vowel or Diphthong. To the Vowel;

Of Double and Treble Rhyme.

LL Words that are accented on the laſt ſave one, re-

quire Rhyme to begin at the Vowel of that Syllable,

and to continue to the End of the Word ; and this 1s what
we call double Rhyme ; as,

Then all for Women, Painting, Rhyming, Drinking,
Beſides ten thouſand Freaks that ay d in Thinking. Dryd,

But it is convenient to take Notice, that the ancient Poets
did not always obſerve this Rule, and took Care only that
the laſt Syllables of the Words ſhoyld be alike in Sound
without any as on to the Seat of the Accent. 'Thus Nation

and Affection, { enderneſs and Hapleſs, Villany and. Gentry, ]
Follew and Willow, and the like, were allow'd as Rhymes to 8
each other, in the Days of Chaucer, Spencer, and the reſt of C
the Ancients; but this is now become a Fault in our Verſifca- b
tion; and theſe two Verſes of Corley rbyme not at all 0

of Evy, nor on the laſt ſave one of znzavigable ; which ne- ti
vertheleſs we muſt be oblig'd to do, if we — the hy ſt of *

Where, unleſs we pronounce the Particle A with a ſtrong
Accent upon it, pe make it found like the Vowel à in the
laſt Syllable but one of Eccle/raftick, the Verſe will loſe all
its Beauty and Rhyme. But this is allowable in Burleſque
Poetry only.

Obſerve that-theſe double Rhymes may be compos'd of
two ſeveral Words, provided the Accent be on the laſt Syl-
4 of the firſt of them; as theſe Verſes of Convey, ſpeaking
of Gold ; |

A Curſe on him who did refine it,
A Curſe on him who finſt did coin it.

Or ſome of the Verſes may end in an entire Word, and
the Rhyme to it be compos'd of ſeveral; as,

Tho flor'd with Deletery Med" cines,
Which whoſoever took is dead ſince. , Hud.

The Treble Rhyme is, when in Words accented on the
laſt ſave two, we begin the Rhyme at the Vowel of that
Syllable, and continue it to the End of the Word: Thus
Charity and Parity, Tenderneſs and Slendernefs, &c. are Tre-
ble Rhymes: and theſe too, as well as the Double, may be
compos'd of ſeveral Words ; as,

There was an ancient ſage Phil:fopher, -
That had read Alexander Roſs over.

The Treble Rhyme is very ſeldom us'd, and ought wholly
to be exploded from ſerious Subjects; for it has a certain
Flatneſs unworthy the Gravity requir'd in Heroick Verſe. In
which Dryden was of Opinion, that even the DoubleRhymes
oughtwery cautiouſly to find Place; and in all his Tranſla-
tions of Virgil he has made uſe of none, except only in ſuch
Words as admit of a Contraction, and therefore cannot pro-
perly be ſaid to be Double Rhymes; as Giw'n, Driv'n, Tow'r,
Peso 'r, and the like. And indeed, conſidering their Meaſure
is different from that of an Heroick Verſe, which conſiſts
but of ten Syllables, they ought not to be too frequently us'd
in Heroick Poems; but they are very graceful in the Ly-
rick, to which, as well as to the Burlefgue, thoſe Rhymes
more properly belong.

Farther Inſtructious concerning Rhyme.

HE Conſonants that precede the Vowels where the

Rhyme begins, muſt be different in Sound, and not-
the ſame; for then the Rhyme will be too perfect ; as Light,
Delight ; Vice, Advice, and the like; for tho' ſuch Rhymes
were allowable in the Days -of Spencer, and the other old
Poets. tl ey are not ſo now, nor can there be any Muſick in
one ſingle Note. Convley himſelf owns, that they ought not
to be allow'd except in Pindarick Odes, which is a Sort of
free Poetry, and there too, very ſparingly, and not without
a Third Rhyme to anſwer to both ; as,

In barren Age æcild and inglorious bye,
And boaſt of paſt Fertility,
The poor Rehief of preſent Poverty. Cowl.

Where the Words Fertility and Poverty rhyme very well
to the laſt Word of the firſt Verſe, Lye ; but cannot rhyme
to each other, becauſe the Conſonants that precede the lak
Vowels are the ſame, both in Writing and Sound.

But this is yet leſs allowable, if the Accent be on the laſt
Syllable of the Rhyme; as,

Her Language melts Omnipotence, arrefls
His Hand, and thence the vengeful Light ning wreſts. Blac.

From hence it follows, that a Word cannot rhyme to its
ſelf tho' the Signification be different; as, He leaves, to thr
Leaves, &c.

Nor the Words that differ both in Writing and Senſe, if
they have the ſame Sound, as Maid and made, Prey and pray,

to bow and à Bough; as,

Hoa gaudy Fate may be in Preſents lent,
And creep inſenſible by Toueh or Scent. Oldh.

Nor a Compound to its Simple; as Move to Remove, Taught
to Untaught, &c.

Nor the Compounds of the fame Words to one another,
as Diſprove to Approve, and the like. All which proceeds from
what 1 ſaid before, viz. That the Conſonants that precede

the Vowels where the Rhyme begins, muſt not be the ſame
in

Word of the Firſt; as,

Encrisn VERSE. ."

in Sound, but different. In all which we vary from our
Neighbours ; for neither the French, Italians, nor Spaniards,
will allow, that a Rhyme can be too perfect: And we meet
with frequent Examples in their Poetry, where not only
the Compounds rhyme to their Simples, and to themſelves ;
but even where Words written and pronounc'd exactly aliks,
provided they have a different Signification, are made uſe of
as Rhymes to another: But this is not permitted in our
Poetry.

We muſt take care not to place a Word at the Middle of a
Verſe that rhymes to the laſt Word of it; as,

So young in ſhow, as if he flill ſhould grow;

But this Fault is ſtill more inexcuſable, if the Second Verſe
rhyme to the Middle and End of the firſt ; as,

Knowledge he only ſought, and fo ſoon caught,

As if fer him Knowledge had rather fought. Cowl,
Her Paſſion ſways, but there the Muſe ſhall raiſe
Eternal Monuments of louder Praiſe. Wall,

Or both the Middle and End of the Second to the laſt

Farewell, fe cry'd, my Siſter, thou dear Part,
Thou fweeteff Part of my divided Heart. Dryd.

Where the Tenderneſs of Expreſſion will not atone for
the Jingle.

Of the Poems composd in Couplets.

N the Poems compos'd in Couplets, the Rhymes follow

the 5th, and in like manner to the End of the Poem,

he Verſes employ'd in this Sort of Poems, are either

O faireſt Piece of well-form'd Earth,
Why-urge you thus your haughty Birth ?
The Pow'r, which you have o er us, lies
Net in your Race, but in your Eyes.

Smile but on me, and you ſhall ſcorn

Henceforth to be of Princes born :

I can deſeribe the ſhady Grove,

I hers your tev'd Mother flept with Jove;

And yet excuſe the faultleſs Dame,

Caught with her Spouſe's Shape and Name :

Thy matchleſs Form will Credit bring

To all the Wonders I all ſing. Wall.

Or of 7; as,

Phillis, avby ſbou/d wwe delay
Pleaſures ſhorter than the Day?
Could abe, which we never can,
Stretch our Lives beyond their Span,
Beauty like a Shadow flies,

And our Youth before us dies.

Or would Youth and Beauty ſtay,
Lowe has Wings, and will away.
Love bas fwifter Wings than Time.

What ſball I de to be for ever known,

Of the Poems compos'd in Stanzas : And firſt of the
Stanzas conſiſting of three, and of four Verſes.

N the Poems compos'd of Stanzas, each Stanza contains
a certain Number of Verſes, conſiſting for the molt part
of a different Number of Syllables : And a Poem that con-
fiſts of ſeveral Stanzas, we generally call an Ode; and this 1s
Lyrick Poetry.

But we muit not forget to obſerve, that our ancient Poets

frequently made uſe of intermix*'d Rhyme in their Heroick

Poems, which they diſpos'd into Stanzas and Cantos. Thus
the Troilus and Crefſida of Chaucer is compos'd in Stanzas
conliſting of 7 Verſes ; the Fairy- Queen of Spencer in Stanzas
of g, Sc. And this they took from the /ta/ians, whole
Heroick Poems generally conſiſt in Stanzas of 8. But this
is now wholly laid afide, and Dawenant, who compos'd his
Gondibert in Stanzas of 4 Verſes in alternate Rhyme, was
the laſt that follow'd their Example of intermingling Rhymes
in Heroick Poetry. | L

The Stanzas employ'd in our Poetry, cannot conſiſt of lefs
than three, and are ſeldom of more than 12 Verles, except
in Pindarick Odes, where the Stanzas are different from one
another in Number of Verſes, as ſhall be ſnewn.

But to treat of all the different Stanzas that are employ'd
or may be admitted in our Poetry, would be a Labour no
leſs tedious than uſeleſs; it being eaſy to demonſtrate, that
they may be vary'd almoſt to an Infinity, that would be dif-
ferent from one another, either in the Number of the Verſes
of each Stanza, or in the Number of the Syllables of each
Verſe ; or laſtly, in the various intermingling of the Rhyme.
I ſhall therefore confine * to mention only ſuch as _

Of the Stanzas of fix Verſes,

HE Stanzas of ſix Verſes, are generally only one of
the before-mention'd Quadrans or Stanzas of Four

another ; as, |

To plead for that which was ſo juſtly giv'n,
To the bright Carliſle of the Courts of Heaw'n.

Of the Stanzas of ten and of twelve Verſes.

HE Stanzas of 10 and 1 2 Verſes are feldom employ d
in our Poetry, it being very difficult to confine our-
ſelves to a certain Diſpoſition of Rhyme, and Meaſure of
Verſe, for ſo many Lines together ; for which Reaſon thoſe
of 4, 6 and 8 Verſes are the moſt frequent. However we ſome
times find ſome of 10 and 12; as in Cowley's Ode, which.
he calls Verſes loft upon a Wager, where the Rhymes follow.
one another; but the Verſes differ in Number of Syllables.

The ſame Poet furniſhes us with an Example of a Stanza
of 12 Verſes in the Ode he calls The Prophet, where the

Rhymes are obſerv'd in the ſame Manner as in the former
Examples, .

Teach me to love ! Go teach thy ſelf Nit:
1 chief Profefſor am of it. ;
Teach Craft to Scots, and Thrift to Jews,

Teach Boldneſs to the Steal.

In Tyrants Courts teach ſupple Flattery,
Teach Feſuits that have travel d far to lie,
Teach Fire to burn, and Winds to blow,

Teach refileſs Fountains how to flow,
Teach the dull Earth fixt to abide,

Teach Womankind Inconflancy and Pride.

See if your Diligence there will uſeful prove ;
But prithee teach not me to love.

ECT. VI.

Of the Stanzas that conſiſt of an odd Number of

E have alſo Stanzas that conſiſt of odd Numbers of
Verſes, as of 5, 7,9, and 11; in all which it of
neceſſity follows, that three Verſes of the Stanza rhyme to
one another, or that one of them be a blank Verſe,
In the Stanzas of 5 Verſes the 1ſt and zd may rhyme,
and the 2d and two laſt; as,

Time what we forbear devours. Wall.

The Stanzas of q and of 11 Verſes are not fo frequent

uſe of many exploded Words: Nor has he, I think, been fol-
low'd in it by any of the Moderns, whoſe 6 firſt Verſes of
the Stanzas that conſiſt of , are generally in Rhymes that
follow one another, and the three laſt a Triplet ; as,

Beauty, Love's Scene and Maſquerade,
So awell by well-plac'd Lights, and Diflance made;
Falſe Coin! with which th Impoſlor cheats us flill,
The Stamp and Colour good, but Metal ill :
Which light or baſe we find, when we
Weigh by Enjoyment, and examine thee.
For tho" thy Being be but Show,
'Tis chiefly Night which Men to thee allow,
And chuſe Penjoy thee, when thou leaſt art thou. Cowl,

SECT. vn.
Of Pindarick Odes, and Poems in Blank Verſe,

HE Stanzas of Pindarick Odes are neither confin'd to
a certain Number of Verſes, nor the Verſes to a cer-
tain Number of Syllables, nor the Rhymes to a certain Diſ.
tance. Some Stanzas contain 50 Verſes or more, others not
above ro, and ſometimes not ſo many: Some Verſes 14,
nay, 16Syllables, others not above 4: Sometimes the Rhymes
follow one another for ſeveral Couplets together, ſometimes
they are remov'd 6 Verſes from each other; and all this in
the ſame Stanza. Cowley was the firſt who introduc'd this
ſort of Poetry into our Language: Nor can the Nature of it
be better deſcrib'd than as he himſelf has done it, in one of
the Stanzas of his Ode upon Liberty, which I will tranſcribe,
not as an Example, for none can properly be given where
no Rule can be preſcrib'd, but to give an Idea of the Na-
ture of this ſort of Poetry. |

1f Life ſhould a well-order*d Poem be,

In which he only hits the White,

Who joins true Profit with the beſi Delight ;
The more heroick Strain let others take,

Mine the Pindarick Way Til male:
The Matter fhall be grave, the Numbers looſe and free ;
Tt ſhall not keep one ſettled Pace of Time,

In the ſame Tune it ſhall not always chime,

Nor Shall each Day juft to his Neighbour rhyme,

A thouſand Liberties it ſhall diſpenſe,

And yet ſhall manage all without Offence,

Or to the Sqweetneſs of the Sound, or Greatneſs of the Sen/e,
Nor hall it ever from one Subject flart,

Nor jeek Tranſitions to depart ;

Nor its ſet Way o'er Stiles and Bridges make,

Nar thro' Lanes a Compaſs take,

EN OLISH VERSE.

As i it fear'd ſome Treſpaſs to commit,
hen the <wide Air a Road for it.
So the Imperial Eagle does not flay
'Till the whole Carcaſs be devour,
That's fall'n into bis Power,
As if his gen r Hunger under/lood,
That he can neuer «vant Plenty of Food;
He only ſucks the taffeful Blood,

And to freſh Game flies chearfully away,
To Kites — meaner Birds he laaves the mangled Prey,

This ſort of Poetry is employ'd in all manner of Subjects;
in Pleaſant, in Grave, in Amorous, in Heroick, in Philoſo-
phical, in Moral, and in Divine.

Blank Verſe is where the Meaſure is exactly kept with-
out Rhyme ; Sbadeſpear, to avoid the troubleſome Conſtraint
of Rhyme, was the firſt who invented it; our Poets ſince

him have made uſe of it in many of their Tragedies and

Comedies: But- the moſt celebrated Poem in this Kind of

Verſe is Milton's Paradiſe loft ; from the 5th Book of which

£ have taken the following Lines for an Example of Blank
erſe. 5 | ö

d when high Noon haſt gain d, and when thou fall g.

In Myſflick Dance, not without Song, reſound

His Praiſe, who out of Darkneſs call'd up Light.

Air] and ye Elements { the eldeſt Birth

Of Nature's Womb, that in Duaternion run
erpetual Circle multiform and mix

And nouriſh all Things ; let your ctaſeleſs Change

Vary to our great Maker flill new Praiſe.

Ye Mis and Exhalations ! that now viſe

From Hill or flanding Lake, duſty or gray,

"Till the Sun paint your fleccy Shirts with Gold,

In Honour to the World's great Author riſe ;

Whether to deck with Clouds th'\uncolour'd Sky,

Or wet the thirfly Earth with falling Show'rs,

Riſing or falling, ill advance his Praiſe.

His Praiſe, ye Winds ! that from four Quarters blow,

Breathe fa T or loud; and wave your Tops, ye Pina !

With er Plant, in Sign of: Worſhip, wave.

Fountains ! and ye that warble as you flow

Melodious Murmurs, warbling tune his Praiſe.

Jin Voices all ye living Souls, ye Birds !

That finging, up to Heav'ns high Gate aſcend,

Bear on your Wings, and in your Notes his Praiſe.

Ye that in Waters glide! and ye that walk

The Earth and flately tread, or lowly creep ;

Witneſs if I be filent, Een or Alorn,

To Hill or Valley, Fountain, or freſh Shade,

Made Vocal by my Song, and taught his Praiſe.

Thus I have given a ſhort Account of all the Sorts of
Poems that are moſt us'd in our Language. The Acroſticks,
Anagrams, &c. deſerve not to be mention'd, and we may
ſay of them what an ancient Poet ſaid long ago,

ABSENCE. See Parting.

Mourn in Abſence, Love's eternal Night. Dr. Pal. £5 Arc.
It was not kind,
To leave me like a Turtle, here alone,
To droop, and mourn the Abſence of my Mate.
When thou art from me, ev'ry Place is detert,
And I, methinks, am ſavage and forlorn.
Thy Preſence only 'tis can make me bleſs'd,
Heal my unquiet Mind, and tune my Soul. Orv, Orph,
Love reckons Hours for Months, and Days for Years :

And ev'ry little Abſence is an Age. Du. Ampbit,
The tedious Hours move heavily away, |
And cach long Minute * lazy Day. Otw. Cai. Mar.

For

50 Abſence, Advice.

For thee the bubbling Springs appear'd to mourn,

And whiſp'ring Pines Voie thy Return. Dryd. Virg,
Night mult involve-the World till ſhe appear ;

The Flow'rs in painted Meadows hang their Heads ;

The Birds awake not to their Morning Songs,

Nor early Hinds renew their conſtant Labour:

Ev'n Nature ſeems to ſlumber till her Call,

Regardleſs of th' Approach of any other Day. RoweUly/.

inds murmur'd thro' the Leaves your ſhort Delay,
And Fountains o'er their Pebbles chid your Stay :
But, with your Preſence chear'd, they ceaſe to mourn! (Inn.
And Walks wear freſher Green at your Return. Dryd. State of
The Joys of Meeting pay the Pangs of Abſence,
Elſe who could bear it ?

When thy lov'd Sight ſhall bleſs my Eyes again,
Then will I own I ought not to complain, (Tam.
Since that ſweet Hour is worth wholeYears of Pain. Row.

I charge thee loiter not, but haſte to bleſs me;
Think with what eager Hopes, what Rage, I burn;
For ev'ry tedious Minute how I mourn !

Think how I call thee cruel for thy Stay, (Uh.
And break my Heart with Grief for thy unkind Delay! Row,
Fly ſwift, ye Hours, you meaſure Time for me in vain,

Till you bring back Leonidas again:
Be ſwifter now; and, to redeem that Wrong,
When he and J are met, be twice as long. Dr. Mar. A-/a-mode.

I grow a God | ſo bleſt, ſo bleſt am I,
With ſacred Rapture, and immortal Joy
But abſent if ſhe ſhines no more,
And hides the Sun that I adore,

AGEON.
Ageon, when with Heav'n he ſtrove,
Stood oppoſite in Arms to mighty Jove;
Mov'd all his hundred Hands, provok'd the War,
Defy'd the forky Lightning from afar ; 2
At fifty Mouths his flaming Breath expires,
And Flaſh for Flaſk returns, and Fires for Fires:
In his right Hand 2s many Swords he wields,
And takes the Thunder on as many Shields. Dryd. Vg.
The Monſter Jian came: |
Thro' wand'ring Skies enormous ſtalk'd along,
Not he that ſhakes the ſolid Earth fo ſtrong :
With Giant Pride at Fove's high Throne he Rands, :
And brandiſh'd round him all his hundred Hands. Pope Hom.
Briareus call'd in Heav'n, but mortal Men below
By his terreſtrial Name Zgeon know. Dryd. Hom,

AOLUS. See Winds. Storm.
The God who does in Caves conſtrain the Winds,
Can with a Breath their clam'rous Rage appeaſe,
They fear his Whiſtle, and forſake the Seas.
Yet once indulg'd, they ſweep the Main,
Deaf to the Call, or hearing, hear in vain.
They, bent on Miſchief, bare the Waves before,
And, not content with Seas, inſult the Shore ;
When Ocean, Air and Earth, at once engage,
And rooted Foreſts fly before their Rage,
At once the claſhing Clouds to Battle move,
And Lightnings run acroſs the Fields above.
In Times of Tempeſt they command alone,
And he but ſits precarious on the Throne. Dryd. Owe,
| Holus, to whom the King of Heav'n,
The Power of Tempeſts and of Winds has giv'n ;
Whoſe Force alone their Fury can reſtrain, ;
And ſmooth the Waves, or ſwell the troubled Main. Dryd. Vir.
His Pow'r to hollow Caverns is confin'd ;
There let him rage the Jailor of the Wind ;
With hoarſe Command his breathing Subjects call, .
And boaſt and bluſter in his empty Hall. 0% Firs.

A TN A,

Mount tna thence we ſpy,
Known by the ſmoaky Flames which cloud the Sky :
By turns a pitchy Cloud ſhe rolls on high ;
By turns hot Embers from her Entrails fly,
And Flakes of Mounting Flames that lick the Sky.
Oft from her Bowels maily Rocks are thrown,
And, ſhiver'd by the Force, come piecemeal down :
Oft liquid Lakes of burning Sulphur flow,
Fed from the fiery Springs that boil below.
Enceladus, they ſay, transfix'd by Powe,
With blafted Wings came tumbling from above;
And where he fell th'avenging Father drew
This flaming Hill, and on lus Body threw :
As often as he turns his weary Sides, | (Pirg.
He ſhakes the ſolid Iſle, and Smoke the Heavens hides. Dryd,
Here preſs'd Enceladus with mighty Loads,
Vomits Revenge in Flames againſt the Gods:
Thro #tna's — he impudently threats,
And thund'ring Heaven with equal Thunder beats. Cr. Lucr.
So Contraries on Ztna's Top conſpire,
Here hoary Froſts, and by them — out Fire.
A Peace ſecure the faithful Neighbours keep;
Th' imbolden'd Snow next to the Flame does ſleep. Coul.
As when the Force
Of ſubterranean Wind tranſports a Hill,
Torn from Pels:u:, or the — Side
Of thund ring tna, whoſe com buſtible
And fuel'd Entrails thence conceiving Fire,
Sublim'd with min'ral Fury, aid the Winds,
And leave a ſinged Bottom all involy'd
With Stench and Smoke. |

Milt,
The Four AGES of the World.

GOLDEN AGE.

The Golden Age was firt, when Man, yet new,

No Rule but uncorrupted Reaſon knew;

And with a native Bent did Good purſue,

Unforc'd by Puniſhment, unaw'd by Fear,

His Words were fimple, and his Soul ſincere :
Needleſs was written Law, where none oppreſs'd ;
The Law of Man was written in his Breaſt,

Seen

2

'd,

wh,

lil.

No Court erected yet, nor Cauſe was heard;

But all was ſafe. Gr Conſcience was their Guard.
The Mountain Trees in diſtant Proſpect pleaſe,

E'er yet the Pine deſcended to the Seas;

E'er Sails were ſpread new Oceans to explore,

And happy Mortals, unconcern'd for more,
Confin'd their Wiſhes to their native Shore.

No Walls were yet, nor Fence, nor Moat, nor Mound ;
Nor Drum was heard, nor 'Trumpet's angry Sound ;
Nor Swords were forg'd : But, void of Care and Crime,
The ſoft Creation ſlept away their Time,

The teeming Earth yet guiltleſs of the Plough,

And unprovok'd, did fruitful Stcres allow.

Content with Food which Nature freely bred,

On Wildings and on Strawberries they fed ;

Cornels and Bramble-berries gave the reſt,

And falling Aeorns furniſh'd out a Feait,

The Flow'rs unfown in Fields and Meadows reign'd,
And Weſtern Winds immortal Spring maintain'd,

In following Years the bearded Corn enſu'd

From Earth unaſk'd, nor was that Earth renew'd,
From Veins of Vallies [.1ilk and Nectar broke,

And tioney ſweated thro' the Pores of Oak.

SILVER AGE.

But when good Saturn, baniſh'd from above,
Was dtivn to Hell, the World was under ove :
Succeeding Times a Silver Age behold,

Excelling Braſs, but more excell'd by Gold:
re Summer, Autumn, Winter, did appear,

nd Spring was but a Seaſon of the Year.

The Sun his annual Courſe, obliquely, made,

Good Days contracted and enlarg'd the Bad.

The Air with ſultry Heits began to glow ;

The Wings of Winds were clog'd with Ice and Snow;
And ſhiv'ring Mortals, into Houſes driv'n,

Sought Shelter from th' Inclemency of Heav'n.
Their Houſes then were Caves, or homely Sheds,
With twining Oziers fenc'd, and \/ofs their Beds.
Then Ploughs for Seed the fruittul Furrows broke,
And Oxen labour'd firſt beneath the Yoke.

54 The four Ages of the World.

BRAZEN AGE.
To this came next in Courſe the Brazen Age;
A warlike Offspring prompt to bloody Rage,
Not impious yet.

- AW wad

'R-Q-N. 4G E.
Hard Steel ſucceeded then,
And ſtubborn as the Metal were the Men.
Truth, Modeſty, and Shame, the World forſook ;
Fraud, Avarice, and Force, their Places took:
'T hen Sails were ſpread to ev'ry Wind that blew ;
Raw were the Sailors, and the Depths were new.
'{rees rudely hollow'd did the Waves ſuſtain,
E'er Ships in Triumph plough'd the wat'ry Main.
Then Land-marks limited to each his Right; ;
For all before was common as the Light :

Nor was the Ground alone requir'd to bear
Her annual Income to the crooked Share : 4
But greedy Mortals, rummaging her Store, ]
Dig'd from her Entrails firſt the precious Ore ;
(Which next to Hell the prudent Gods had laid)

And that alluring Ill to Sight difplay'd :
Thus curſed Ste, and — — Gold,

Gave Miſchief Birth, and made that Miſchief bold;

And double Death did wretched Man invade, 1 N
By Steel aſſaulted, and by Gold betray d. 1 n
Now brandiſh'd Weapons glitt'ring in their Hands,
Mankind is broken looſe from mortal Bands. q
No Rights of Hoſpitality remain; |

The Gueſt, by him that harbour'd him, is ſlain :
"The Son-in- Law purſues the Father's Life;

The Wife her Huſband murders, he the Wife: :
The Stex-dame Poiſon for the Son prepares;

The Son, enquires into his Father's Years :

Faith flies. and Piety in Exile mourns :

And 7#/lice, here oppreſs'd, to Heav'n returns. D/. Ovid.

SILVER AGE. |
E'er this no Pea/ant vex'd the peaceful Ground,
Which only Turfs and Greens for Altars found:
No Fences parted Fields; nor Marks, nor Bounds
Liſtinguiſh'd Acres of litigious Grounds:

ewe added Venom to the Viper's Brood,

And ſwell'd with raging Storms the peaceful Flood;
Commiſſion'd hungry Wolves t'infeſt the Fold,

And ſhook from oaken Leaves the liquid Gold :
Remov'd from human Reach the chearful Fire,
And from the Rivers bade the Wine retire ;

That ftudious Need might uſeful Arts explore

From furrow'd Fields to reap the foodful Store,
And force the Veins of yrs een Flints t'expire

The lurking Seeds of their celeſtial Fire.

Then firſt on Seas the hollow'd Alder ſwam :

Then Sailors quarter'd Heav'n, and found a Name
For ev'ry fixt, and ev'ry wand'ring Star,

The Pleiadt, Hyads, and the Northern Car. |
Then Toils for Beaſts, and Lime for Birds were ur d,
And deep-mouth'd Dogs did Foreſt-Walks ſurround ;
And Caſting Nets were ſpread in hollow Brooks,
Drags in the Deep, and Baits were hung on Hooks ;
Then Saws were tooth'd, and ſounding Axes made,
And various Arts in Order did — o Dryd. Virg.

Future GOLDEN AGE,

Unbidden Earth ſhall wreathing Ivy bring,
And fragrant Herbs, the Promiſes of Spring :
The Goats with ſtruttiag Duggs ſhall homeward ſpeed,
And lowing Herds ſecure from Lions feed.
The Serpent's Brood ſhall die: the ſacred Ground
Shall Weeds and pois' nous Plants refuſe to bear,
Each common Buth ſhall Syrian Roſes wear:
Unlabour'd Harveſts ſhall the Fields adorn,
And cluſter'd Grapes ſhall bluſh on ev'ry Thorn.
The knotted Oak ſhall Show'rs of Honey weep,
And thro' the matted Graſs the liquid Gold ſhall creep.
The I. Sailor ſhall the Seas —
No Keel ſhall cut the Waves for foreign Ware,
For ev'ry Soil ſhall ev'ry Product bear.
The lab'ring Hind his Oxen ſhall disjoin,
No Plough ſhall hurt the Glebe, no Pruning-hook the Vine,
Nor Woot ſhall in diſſembled Colours ſhine :
But the luxurious Father of the Fold,
With native Purple, or unborrow'd Gold,

The Virgin Daughter of eternal Night :

Ev'n luto hates his Cen miſ-ſhapen Race,
Her Siſter L 72s fiv her hideous Face,

is hers, to ruin Realms, -o'erturn a State,
Betwixt the dearett Friends to raiſe Debate,
And kindle Kindred blood to mutual Hate.
Her Hand o'er Towns the fun'ral Torch diſplays,
And forms a thouſand Ills ten thouſand Ways.
She ſhakes from out her fruitful Breaſt the Seeds
Of Envy, Diſcord, and of cruel Deeds :
Confounds eſtabliſh'd Peace, and does prepare
Their Souls to Hatred, and their Hands to War. DHd. Virg.

The Fates infernal Miniſter!

War, Death, Deſtruction, in her Hand ſhe bears;
Her curling Snakes with Hiſſings fill the Place,
And open all the Furies of her Face.
Her Chains ſhe rattles, and her Whips ſhe ſhakes,

Churning her bloody Foam, Dq d. Jing.

AMAZON.

So march'd the Thracian Amazons of old,
When 7 kermiudon with bloody Billows roul'd :
Such Troops as theſe in ſhining Arms were ſeen,
When 7 heſevs met in Fight their Maiden Queen,
Such to the Field Penthefilea led,

From the fierce Virgin when the Grecians fled :
With ſuch return'd triumphant from the War ;

Her Maids with Crics attend the lofty Car: 'T bey

Amazon. Ambition. 57

They claſh with manly Force their moony Shields

With Female Shouts reſound the Pian Fields. Dryd. Firg.
Reſiſtleſs thro' the War Camilla rode.

In Danger unappall'd, and pleas'd with Blood.

One Side was bare for her exerted Breaſt,

One Shoulder with her painted Quiver preſs'd.

Now from afar her fatal Jav'lins play;

Now with her Axe's Edge ſhe hews her Way.

Diana's Arms upon her Shoulders ſound ; | 1

And when too eloſely preſs'd, ſhe quits the Ground, ¶Virg.

From her bent Bo ſhe ſends a backward Wound. Dryd. )
Penthefilea there, with haughty Grace,

Leads to the War an Amazonian Race:

In their right Hands a pointed Dart they wield ;

Their left, for Ward, ſuſtains the lunar Shield.

Athwart her Breaſt a golden Belt ſhe throws ; {

— wv

Amidit the Prefs, alone, provokes a thouſand Foes, (Virg.
And dares her maiden Arms to manly Force oppoſe. Dryd.
1he little Amazon could hardly go,
He loads her with a Quiver and a Bow;
And, that ſhe might her ſtaggering Steps command,
He with a ſlender Jav'lin fills her Hand :
Her flowing Hair no golden Fillets bound,
Nor ſwept her trailiag Kobe the duſty Ground.
Inſtead of theſe a Tiger's Hide o'erſpread
Her Back and Shoulders, faſten'd to her Head.
The flying Dart ſhe firſt attempts to fling, -
And round her tender Tempies toſs'd the Sling.
Then, as her Strength with Years increas'd began
To pierce aloft in Air the ſoating Swan,
And from the Clouds to fetch the Heron and the Crans.

58 Ambition. Angel.

But oh! the Curſe of aiming to be great!

Dazzled with Hope we cannot ſee the Cheat.

When wild Ambition in the Heart we find,

Farewe! Content, and Quiet of the Mind:

For gict'ring Cloods we leave the ſolid Shore,

And wouted Haprineis returns no more. Har, Juv,
But wild Ambitton loves to flide, not ftand ;

And Fortnne's Ice prefers to Virtue's Land. Dr. ALF. & Ach,
Yet true Renown is fill with Virtue join'd,

But Luft of Pow ' lets looſe the th*unbridled Mind. Dryd. Auren,
Ambition! the Defre of active Souls,

That puſhes them beyond the Bounds of Nature,

And elevates the 1ero to the Gods, Row. Anb. Step.
O Energy divine of great Ambition!

That can inform the Souls of beardleſs Boys,

And ripen em to Men in ſpite of Nature. Row. Amb. Step.
Ambition is like Love, impatient

Both of Delays and Rivals. Denb. Soph.
Ambition never ſafe, till Pow'r be paſt :

As Men, till impotent, are ſeldom chaſte. Sedl. Ant. & Cle.
Ambition is the Dropſy of the Soul, & Cic:p.

Whole Thirſt we muſt not yield to, but controul. Seal. Ant.
If Glory was a Bait that Angels ſwallow'd,

How then ſhould Souls, ally'd to Senſe, reſiſt it ? — Sec.
ove.

One World ſuffic'd not Alexander's Mind,

Coop'd up, he ſeem'd, in Eaith and Seas confin'd:

And, ſtruggling, ſtretch'd his reſtleſs Limbs about

The narrow Globe to find a Paſſage out:

Yet enter'd in the Brick-built Town, he try'd

The Tomb, and found the ſtreight Dimenſions wide.

Death only this myſterious Truth unfolds,

The mighty Soul how ſmall a Body holds! Dryd. Jud.

The Blaſt which his ambitious Spirit ſwell'd,

See by how weak a Tenure it was held | Dryd. Aurer.
Ambition's like a Circle on the Water

Which never ceaſes to enlarge i:ſelf,

Till by broad ſpreading, it diſperſe to nought. Shak. Hen. VI.
For Kiugs oft loſe the Conqueſts gain'd before,

By vain Ambition ſtill to make them more. Pope,
Vaulting Ambition Kill o'erleaps itſelf. Shak, Macb,

ANGEL.
Then Gabriel

Bodigs and clothes himlelf, with thicken'd Air, All

LY

Angel. 59

All like a comely Youth in Life's freſh Bloom,

Rare Workmanſhip, and wrought by heav'nly Loom!

He took for Skin a Cloud moſt foft and bright,

That e'er the mid-day Sun pierc'd thro' with Light.

Upon his Cheeks a lively Bluſh he ſpread,

Waſh'd from the Morning Beauties deepeſt Red.

A harmleſs flaming Meteor ſhone for Hair,

And fell adown his Shoulders with looſe Care.

He cut out a Silk Mantle from the Skies,

Vhere the moſt ſprightly Azure pleaſe the Eyes :

T his he with ftarry Vapours ſpangles, all

Ta'en in their Prime, e'er they grow ripe and fall.

Of a new Rainbow, e'er it fret or fade,

The choiceſt Piece ta'en out, a Scarf is made.

Small ſtreaming Clouds he does for Wings diſplay ;

Nor virtuous Lovers ſigh more ſoſt than they:

Theſe he gilds o'er with the Sun's richeſt Rays,

Caught gliding o'er pure Streams, on which he plays.
Thus dreſs'd he poſts away,

And carries with him his own glorious Day,

Thro' the thick Woods: The gloomy Shades awhile

Put on freſh Looks, and wonder why they ſmile.

The trembling Serpents cloſe and filent lie;

The Birds obſcene far from his Paſlage fly.

A ſadden Spring waits on him as he goes,

Sudden as that by which Creation roſe. Cort,
Down thither prone-in Flight,

He ſpeeds, and thro' the vaſt ethereal Sky,

Sails between Worlds and Worlds with ſteady Wings ;

Now on the Polar Winds, then with quick Fan

Winnows the buxom Air.

Of beamy ſunny Rays a golden Tiar

Circled his Head, nor leſs his Locks behind

Illuſtrious on his Shoulders, fledg'd with Wings,

Lay waving round. |

Six Wings he wore to ſhade

His Liniments divine: The Pair that clad

Each Shoulder broad, came mantling o'er his Breaſt

With regal Ornament ; the middle Fair

Girt, like a ſtarry Zone, his Wale, and round

Skirted his Loins and Thighs with downy Gold,

And Colours dipt in Heaven : The third; his Feet

Shadow'd from either Heel with feather'd Mail,

Sky- tinctur d Grain, Like Maia's Son he ſtood,

*Fnflamed Rage in glowing Breaſt ;
Which now began to rage and burn as
Implacably, as Flame in Furnace.
He trembled and look'd pale with Tre,
Like Aſhes firit, then red as Fire.
At this the Knight grew high in Wroth,
And lifting Hands and Eyes up both,
Three Tames he ſmote on Stomach ſtout. Bud.
With fiery Eyes, and with contracted Brows,

He coin'd his Face in the ſevereſt Stamp,

And Fury ſhook his Fabrick like an Earthquake,

ANT. See Creation.
Thus in Battalia march embodyed Ants,
Feartul of Winter and of future Wants,
("invade the Corn; and to their Cells convey
The plunder'd Forage of their yellow Prey.
The ſable Troops, along the narrow Tracts,
Scarce bear the wetghty Burden on their Backs:
Some ſet their Shoulders to the pond'rous Grain,
Some guard the _ ſome laſh the lagging Train: |
All ply their ſev'ral Taſks, andequal'Toil ſuſtain. Dyd. irg.
The little Drudge does trot about and ſweat,
Nor will he ſtrait devour all he can
But in his temp'rate Mouth carries it home;
A Stock for Winter, which he knows muſt come. Cowl. Hor.

ANTIQUARY ard ANTIQUITY.

It was a Queſtion whether he

Or's Horſe were of a Family

More Worſhipful ; till Antiquaries |

{After they'd almoſt por'd out their Eyes)

Did very learnedly decide

The Bus'nefs on the Horſe's. Side;

And prov'd not only Horſe, but Cows,

Nay Pigs, were of the elder Houle :

For Beaſts, when Man was but a Piece 48

Of Earth himſelf, did th' Earth poſſeſs. Hud.
"Tis not Antiquity, nor Author,

That makes Truth Truth, altho' Time's Daughter.

*T'was he that put her in the Pit,

Before he pull'd her out of it.

And as he eats his Sons, juſt fo

He feeds upon his Daughters too.

Nor does it follow, *cauſe a- Herald

Can make a Gentleman, ſcarce a Year old,

To be deſcended from a Race

Of ancient Kings, in a ſmall Space:

That we ſhould all Opinions hold 9
Authentick, that we can make old. Hud.

62 Apollo, Apothecary and bis Shop. +

When to his native Deli he reſorts,

Where painted Shin, mix'd with Cretan Bands,

The merry Madneſs of the ſacred Show,

A golden Fillet binds his awful Brows ;
His- Quiver ſounds. Dryd. Virg,

heſe Hands the Paten Sceptre ſway ;
he King of God: begot me: What ſhall be,

APOTHECARY and his Shop.
I do remember an Apothecary,

In tatter'd Weeds, with overwhelming Brows,

Culling of Simples: meagre were lus Looks,

Sharp Miſery had worn him to the Bones;

And in his needy Shop a Tortoiſe hung,

An Alligator ſtuff'd, and other Skins

Of ill-ihap'd Fiſhes, and about his Shelves

A beggarly Account of empty Boxes,

Green earthen Pots, Bladders, and muſty Seeds,
Remnants of Packthread, and old Cakes of Roſes, R
Were thinly ſcattered to make up a Show. Shak. Rom u..
His Shop the gazing V ulgar's Eyes employs
With foreign Trinkets, and domeſtick Toys:
Here Mummies lay, moſt reverendly ſtale,
And there the Tortoiſe hung her Coat of Mail,
Not far from ſome huge Shark's devouring Head,
The flying Fiſh their finny Pinions ſpread ;
Aloft in Rows large Poppy Heads were ſtrung,
And near a ſcaly Alligator hung :

APPLAUSE. See Popular.

Loud as the Surges when the Tempeſt blows,

That. daſh'd on broxen Rocks, tumultuous roar,

And foam and thunder on the ſtony Shore. Pope Hom.

His Army's juſt Applauſes riſe,

And the loud Shout runs echoing thro' the Skies. Pope Hom.

And loud Applauſes of the ſhouting Ihrong. Bac,

ARCHE R. See Arrow. Boa. —
A flutt'ring Dove to the Maſt's Top they tie : A

The living Mark at which their Arrows fly:
The rival Archers in a Line advance;

Then all with Vigour bend their truſty Bows,
And from the Quwer each his Arrow choſe. 0
ny yl was the firſt, with forceful Sway

It flew, and whizzing, cut the liquid Way. 7
Fix'd in the Maſt, the feather'd Weapon ſtands; 1
The fearful- Pigeon flutters in her Bands, 7
And the Tree trembled. B
Then Mne/theus to the Head his Arrow drove, ( 8

With lifted Eyes, and took his Aim above;

But made a glancing Shot, and miſs'd the Dove: ]
Yet miſs'd ſo narrow, that he cut the Cord, "1
Which faſten'd by the Foot the flitting Bird, -

The Captive thus releas'd, away the flies,

And beats, with clapping Wings, the yielding Skies.

His Bow already bent, Euryams ſtood ; |
His winged Shaft with eager Haſte he ſped ;
The fatal Meſlage reach'd her as ſhe fled : |
She leaves her Life aloft, ſhe ſtrikes the Ground,

And renders back the Weapon in the Wound,

Aceſies, grudging at his i ot, remains

Without a Frize to gratiſy his Pains ;

Yet, ſhooting upwards, ſends his Shaft to ſhow

An Archer's Art, and boaſt his twanging Bow.

Chaf'd by the Speed, it fir'd, and as it flew,

A rail of foll wing Flames aſcending drew.

Kindling they uount; aud mark the ſhiny Way;

Acroſs che S y, as failing Meteors play,

And vaniſh into Wind, or in a Blaze decay. Dryd. Ferg.

Argus. Armour. 65

ARGUS.

The Head of Argus, as with stars the Skies,
Was compaſs'd round, and wore a hundred Eyes: ay
Put two by Turns their Lids in Slumber ſteep ; |
The reſt, on Duty till, their Station keep:
Nor could the total Conſtellation fleep.
Him Hermes ſlew ;
And all his hundred Eyes, with all their Light,
Are clos'd at once in one perpetual Night.
Theſe Juno takes, that they no more may fail, |
And ſpreads them in her Peacock's gaudy Tail. Dryd. Ovid.

ARMOUR. See Battle. Fighting. General. Soldier. War.
He ſheath'd his Limbs in Arms, a temper'd Maſs |
Of golden Metal thoſe, and Mountain Braſs.
He admires
The creſted Helm that vomits radiant Fires :
His Hands the fatal Sword and Croflet hold;
One keen with temper'd Steel, one ſtiff: with Gold,
Both ample, flaming both, and beamy bright; x
So ſhines a Cloud, when edg'd with adverſe Light. Dryd. Virg.
Refulgent Arms appear
Redd'ning the Skies, and glitt'ring all around;
The temper'd Metals claſh, and yield a filver Sound. Dryd. Virg.
The Panther's ſpeckled Hide :
Flow'd o'er his Armour with an eaſy Pride. Pop. Hom,
High on his Helm celeſtial Lightnings play;
His beamy Shield emits a living Ray :
14 unweary'd Blaze inceſſant Streams ſupplies,
T ike the red Star, that fires th' autumnal Skies,
Vhen, freſh, he rears his radiant Orb to Sight,
And bath'd in Ocean, ſhoots a keener Light:
Such from his Arms the bright Effulgence flow'd, Pope Hem.
The Briton's Arms thus ſhone exceflive bright,
Jarted keen Glances, and uneaſy Light;
And tho? their Glory pleas'd, it pain'd the Sight. Pac.
All arm'd in Fraſs, the richeſt Dreſs of War;
A frightful glorious Sight he ſhone from far. Coauli.
His ſolid Arms, refalgent, flame with Gold:
No mortal Shoulders ſuit the glorious Load;
Celeſtial Panoply, to grace a God! Pope Hom.
A Wolf grinn'd horribly upon his Head,
And o'er bis brawny Back a Leopard's Hide was * |

66 Armour.

He girt his mighty Fauchion to his Side,

Which hung acrofs his Thigh with fearful Pride. Bla:
Shields, Arms, and Spears flaſh horribly from far,

And the Fields glitter with a waving War. Dryd. Virg
Spears, Helmets, Muſkets with the Sunbeams play,

Their flaſhing Glances thro' the Field convey,

And bandy to and fro reverberated Day. Blac,
Their Swords, their Armous, and their Eyes ſhot Flame.
He on the Plain in radiant Armour ſhown ; { Creech Luc,

His poliſh'd Helm oppreſs d the dazzled Sight,

And ſhone on high hke a huge Globe of Light.

His Coat of Mail was on his Shoulders caſt,

And golden Cuiſhes his vaſt Thighs encas'd.

The Pieces round his Legs Gold Buttons ty'd,

And his broad Sword hung dreadful by his Side;

Which, when drawn out, hke a deſtructive Flame

Of Lightning from the ample Scabbard came. Blac,
Like a huge Beacon lighted in the Air,

His Buckler dam d, denouncing horrid War.

In his right Hand he ſhakes his pond'rous Lance, Bla.

His Back and Breaſt
Well vayorl Steel and ſcaly Braſs inveſt.
The Cuiſhes which his brawny Thighs infold,
Were mingled Metal damaſk'd o'er with Gold,
His faithful Fauchion fits upon his Side,
Nor Caſque nor Creſt his manly Features hide. Dry. Ving.
O'er his broad Breaſt an Ox's Hide was thrown,
His Helm a Wolf, whoſe gaving Jaws were ſpread,
A Cov'ring for his Cheeks, and grinn'd around his Head.

He clench'd within his Hand an Iron Prong, (Vit.
And tow'rd above the reſt, conſpicuous in the Throng, Dry4.
Himſelf before the reſt

His mighty Limbs in radiant Armour dreſt :

And firſt he cas'd his manly Legs around

In ſhining Greaves, with Silver Buckles bound :

The beaming Cuiraſs next adorn'd his Breaſt.

Ten Rows of azure Steel the Work infold,

Twice ten of Tin, and twelve of ductile Gold:

Three glitt'ring Dragons to the Gorget riſe,

Whoſe 1mitated Scales againſt the Skies,

Reflected various Light, and arching bow'd,

Like colour'd Rainbows o'er a ſhow'ry Cloud.

A radiant Bauldrick, 0'er his Shoulder ty'd,

Sultain'd the Sword, that glitter'd at his Side; Gold

Gold was the Hilt; a ſilver Sheath encas'd

The ſhining Blade, and golden Hangers grac'd:

His Buckler's mighty Orb was next diſplay'd,

That round the Warrior caſt a dreadful Shade:

Ten Zones of Braſs its ample Brims ſurround,

And twice ten Eofles the bright Convex crown'&:

Tremendous Gorgon frown'd upon its Field,

And circling Terrors fill'd th' expreſſive Shield:

Within its Concave hung a ſilver Thong,

On which a mimick Serpent cxeeps along,

His azure Length in eaſy Waves extends,

Till in three Heads th' embroider'd Mouſter ends.

Lait, o'er his Brows his fourfold Helm he plac'd,

With nodding Horſe-hair formidably — ;

And in his Hands two ſteely Jav'lins wields,

That blaze to Heav'n, and lighten all the Fields. Poe Hom.

A Lion's Hide he wears, |

About his Shoulders hangs the ſhaggy Skin; i =£1

The Teeth and gaping Jaws ſeverely grin. Ded. J irg.
Some march before the Troops in dreadful Pride,

Arm'd with a rav'ning Lion's griſſy Hide:

The ſhaggy Back was o'er their -- ++ — > nh

With formidable Grace; and on their

The tawny Terror grinn'd with open Jaws,

And croſs the Breaft were lapp'd the hideous Paws.

The Teeth and ſavage Beard the Heroe's Face _

Did with becoming martial Horror grace. Blac.

Some wore Coat- Armour, imitating Scale,

And next their Skin were ſtubborn Shirts of Mail ;

Some wore a Rreaft-plate, and a light Juppon ;_

Their Horſes cloath'd with rich Capariſon.

Some for Defence would Leathern Bucklers ufe,

Of folded Hides ; and other Shields of Pruce.

One hung a Pole ax at his Saddle-bow,

And one a heavy Mace to ſtun the Foe.

One for his Legs and Knees provided well,

Vith Jambeux arm'd, and double Plates of Steel.

This on his Helmet wore a Lady's Glove,

And that a Sleeve embroider'd by his Love.Dryd. Pal & Arc.
Words and Devices blaz'd on ev'ry Shield,

Ang pleaſing was the Terror of the Field. Dryd. Pal. & Arc,

ART. See Nature.
| ASH. See Trees.

Rent like a mountain Aſh that dar'd the Winds,
And ftood the ſturdy Strokes of lab'ring Hinds,
About the Root the cruel Ax reſounds ; X
The Stumps are pierc'd with oft- repeated Wounds :
The War 1s felt on high, the nodding Crown
Now threats a Fall, and throws the leafy Honours down.
To their united Force it yields, tho' late,
And mourns with mortal Groans th' approaching Fate.
The Roots no more their upper Head faſtain,
But down ſhe falls, and ſpreads a Ruin thro' the Plain.

, 4 Dryd. Virg.
Like a mountain Aſh, whoſe Roots are ſprea
Deep fix'd in Earth, in Clouds he hides bis Head, Dryd. Virg.

AS PICK.

Welcome thou kind Deceiver,
Thou beſt of Thieves! who with an eaſy Key
Doſt open Life, and unperceiv'd by us. |
Ev'n ſteal us from ourſelves; diſcharging ſo
Death's dreadful Office better than himſelf ;
Touching our Limbs ſo gently into Slumber,
I hat Death ſtands by, deceiv'd by his own Image,
And thinks himſelf but Sleep. Dryd. All for Love.

Prepare to hear
A Story that ſhall turn thee into Stone:
Could there be hewn a monſtrous Gap in Nature,
A tlaw made thro' the Centre by ſome God,
Thro' which the Groans of Ghoſts might ſtrike thy Ears,
They would not wound thee as this Story will. Lee Oedip.
My Heart {inks in me,
And ev'ry ſlacken'd Fibre drops its Hold,
Like Nature letting down the Springs of Lite. Dryd. Spa. Fry.
My Soul runs back :
The Wards of Reaſon rou! into their Spring. Lee D. of Guiſe,
It drives my Soul back to her inmoſt Seats,
And freezes ev'ry ſtiff ning Limb to Marble. Raw. LU.

ASTROLOGER.. See Conjurer,
They'll ſearch a Planet's Houſe to know

Who broke and robb'd a Houſe below.
Examine Venus and the Moon,
Who ſtole a Thimble, who a Spoon
And tho' they nothing will confeſs,
Yet by their very Looks can gueis,
And tell what guilty Aſpe& bodes,
Who ſtole, and who receiv'd the Goods.
They'll feel the Pulſes of the Stars. |
To find out Agues, Coughs, Catarrhs : And

Aſtrologer.
And tell what Cr:// does divine
The Rot in Sheep, the Mange in Swine :
In Men what gives or cures tune lich,
What makes them Cuckolds, poor Or rich;
Wat gains or toſes, hangs or ſaves;

What makes Men great, what Fools, what Knaves;

Bur not what wile : For only df thoſe

The Stars they ſay, cannot diſpoſe,

No more than can the Aſtrologians;
There they ſay right, and like true Trojans.
Some "towns and Cities, fome, for Brevity,
Have caſt the *verſal World's Nativity,
And made the Infant Stars confeſs,

Like Fools or Children, what they pleaſe.
Some calculate the hidden Fates,

Of Monkeys, Puppy-dogs, and Cats;
Some running Nags, and fighting Cocks :

Some Love, Trade, Law-Snits, and the Pox.

Some take a Meaſure of the Lives
Of Fathers, Mothers, Huſbands, Wives :
Make Oppoſition, trine and quartile,
Tell who is barren, and who fertile,

As if the Planet's firſt Aſpe&

The tender Infant did infe& :

No ſooner had he peep'd into

'The World, but he has done his'Do ;
Catch'd all Diſeaſes, took all Phyfick,
That cures or kills a Man that is ſick;
Marry'd his punctual Doſe of Wives,
Is cuckolded, and breaks or thrives,
There's but the twinkling of a Star
Between a Man of Peace. and War;
A Thief and Juſtice, Fool and Knave,
A huffing Officer and a Slave;

A- crafty Lawyer, and Pick-pocket,
A great Philofopher, and a Blockhead ;
A formal Preacher, and a Player,

A learn'd Phyſician and Man flayer,
As if Men from the Stars did ſuck
Old Age, Diſeaſes, and ill Luck ;
Wit, Folly; Honour, Virtue, Vice,

Trade, Travel, Women, Claps, and Dice:

And draw with the firſt Air they breathe
Battle and Murder, ſudden Death,

Profeſſor in Aſtrology and Phyſick. 71

As Wind i'th' Hypocondries pent,

Is but a Blaſt if downward ſent;

But if it upwards chance to fly,

Becomes new Light and Prophecy:

So when your Speculations tend

Above their juſt and uſeful End,
Although they promiſe ſtrange and great
Diſcoveries of Things far ſer,
They are but idle Dreams aud Fancies.
Tell me but what's the nat'ral Cauſe,
Why on a Sign no Painter draws

The Full-Moon ever, but the Half,
Refolve that with your Jacob's Staff;

Or why Wolves raiſe a Hubbub at her,
Or Dogs howl when ſhe ſhines in Water;
And I hall oy give my Vote,

You may know ſomething more remote. Hud,

PROFESSOR in Afrology and Phy/ict

An inner Room receives the num'rous Shoals
Of ſuch as pay to be reputed Fools :
Globes ſtand on Globes, Volumes on Volumes lie,
And planetary Schemes amuſe the Eye.
The Sage in Velvet-Chair here lolls at Eaſe,
To promiſe Future Health for preſent Fees.
Then, as from Tripod, ſolemn Shams reveals,
And what the Stars know nothing of, foretels.
One aſks, how ſoon Panthea may be won,
And longs to feel the Marriage-Fetters on:
Others, convinc'd by melancholy Proof,
Enquire when courteous Fates will ſtrike 'em off,
dome by what Means they may redreſs the Wrong,
When . athers the Poſſeſſion keep too long.
And ſome would know the Iſſue of their Cauſe,
And whether Gold can folder up its Flaws:
Poor pregnant Lais his Advice would have,
To loſe by Art what fruitful Nature gave ;
And Portia old in Expectation grown,
Laments her barren Curſe, and begs a Son :
Whilſt Iris his coſmetick Waſh would try,
To make her Bloom revive, and Lover die.

Some aſk for Charms, and others Philtres chooſe,

To gain Cori and their Quartans loſe. |

Let all be id; each ſofteſt Motion ceaſe ;

; Be calm, as in the Arms of Death.

Thou reſtleſs Wanderer, my Heart,

Thou bu idle thing to heave:
Stir not a Pulſe; let my Blood,
That turbulent unruly Flood,

AVERNUS. |

Deep was the Cave, and downward as it went
From the wide Mouth, a rocky rough Deſcent.
And here th'Acceſs a gloomy Grove extends,
And there th' I Lake extends,
O'er whoſe unhappy Waters void of Light,
No Bird preſumes to ſteer his airy Flight :
Such deadly Stenches from the Depth ariſe
And ſteaming Sulphur that infects the Skies.
From hence the Grecian Bards their Legends make, -
And give the Name Avernus to the Lake, Dryd. Firg,

BAB E. See Man.
Thus, like a Sailor by the Tempeſt hurl'd
Aſhore, the Babe is ſhipwreck'd on the World;
Naked he lies, and ready to expire,
Helpleſs of all that human Wants require,
Expos'd upon unhoſpitable Earth,
From the firſt Moment of his hapleſs Birth.
Straight with foreboding Cries he fills the Room;
(Too ſure Preſages of his future Doom.)
But Flocks and Herds, and ev'ry ſavage Beaſt,
By more indulgent Nature are increas'd.
They want no Rattles for their froward Mood,
No Nurſe to reconcile them to their Food
With broken Words; nor Winter Blaſts they fear,
Nor change their Habits with the changing Year :
Nor for their Safety Citadels prepare,
Nor forge the wicked Inſtruments of War :
Unlabour'd Earth her bounteous Treaſure grants, { Lucr.
AndNature's laviſhHand ſupplies their common Wants. Dr.
If tender Infants, who impriſon'd ſtay |
Within the Womb, prepar'd to break away,
Were conſcious of themſelves, and of their State,
And had but Reaſon to ſuſtain Debate : 3
The painful Paſſage they would dread, and ſhew
Reluctance to a World they do not know:
They in their Priſons till would chuſe to lie,
As backward to be born as we to die. 5 Blas.

Great Father Bacchzs to my Song repair,
For cluſt'ring Vines are thy pecultar Care : |
For thee large Bunches load the bending Vine;
And the laſt Bleſſings of the Year are thine :
To thee his Joys the jolly Autumn owes,
When the fermenting Juice the Vat o'erflows. |
Come ſtrip with me, my God; come drench all o'er
Thy Limbs in Muſt of Wine, and drink at ev'ry Pore.

| BASTARD. |
Why ſhould dull Law rule Nature, who firſt made
That Law, by whielFherſelf is now betray'd ?
Ere Man's Corruptions made him wretched, he
Was born moſt noble, who was born moſt free:
Each of himſelf was Lord; and unconfin'd,
Obey'd the Dictates of his Godlike Mind.
Law was an Innovation brought in fince,
When Fools began to love Obedience,
And call their Slav'ry Safety and Defence. |
Why ſhould it be a Stain then on me Blood, |
